Key Contribution #36 — Linguistic-Provenance Conformance and the EU-Multilingual Sovereignty Surface: The EU’s 24-language legislative canon makes terminology a verifiable regulatory layer rather than a content choice; the dual-use case (Reg. 2021/821) shows a structural register split between the market-recognised international short form (“dual-use”, as used by Nokia / Patria / VTT / NATO DIANA) and the legally canonical national long forms (kaksikäyttötuotteet / produkter med dubbla användningsområden / Güter mit doppeltem Verwendungszweck / biens à double usage). The contribution names a fifth-style conformance predicate extending the four-track KYA suite — linguistic-provenance conformance, EU AI Act Article 13-adjacent — requiring that a sovereign agent’s published regulatory claims resolve, per official language, to an attestable canonical term of record. Positioned at the 3–7 year horizon (§1.3) because it tracks the EDIP 2026–2027 deployment wave, the NATO DIANA / DEFINE sovereign-C2 pipeline, and the standardisation of a multilingual claim-provenance schema. Primary sources: Regulation (EU) 2021/821 (EUR-Lex); Sweden ISP / Tullverket; Nokia “Defense networks”; VTT “dual-tech companies and defence”.

14.31 The Sovereign-Upstream and Open-Agent-Runtime Signals: EU AI Factories and NVIDIA’s Agent Security Stack

Added: 25.06.2026 | Sources: NVIDIA Newsroom, “Europe Unveils a Record 35 New NVIDIA AI Supercomputers” (22.06.2026); NVIDIA Newsroom, “NVIDIA Announces NemoClaw for the OpenClaw Community”; github.com/NVIDIA/NemoClaw; docs.nvidia.com/nemoclaw; NVIDIA NeMo Agent Toolkit (github.com/NVIDIA/NeMo-Agent-Toolkit, docs.nvidia.com/nemo/agent-toolkit). PhD scope per §1.3 (3–7 year horizon, market signal).

Two NVIDIA signals landed in June 2026 that bear directly on the Firehorse sovereign-edge operating model from opposite ends of the stack — one upstream (where models are trained), one at the agent runtime (where models act). Read together, they sharpen rather than blur the upstream/downstream division that §14.18 (owned-compute hedge) and §14.21 (substrate integrity) established.

14.31.1 The sovereign-upstream signal: EuroHPC AI Factories

On 22 June 2026 NVIDIA reported that 35 new AI/HPC supercomputers are in development across 23 European countries — roughly 800 AI exaflops deployed or announced over the prior year — anchored by EuroHPC AI Factories including Barcelona’s MareNostrum 5 expansion (GB300 NVL72 + GB200 NVL4, on the order of ~20 EF training / ~33 EF inference), BavariaAI, HLRS, and NAISS, built primarily on Blackwell and Hopper silicon and serving 3M+ researchers. The operating-model significance is that the EU-sovereign upstream — the training tier the §14.18 hedge presupposed but could not point to — now has a public, EU-jurisdiction address. It does not collapse the distinction this thesis draws: a shared, research-first national facility is not a single-tenant, air-gapped, Article-12-per-deployer inference substrate inside a regulated operator’s building. It resolves the provenance question (train on EU soil, under EU governance) while leaving the deployment question (CLOUD Act immunity, on-prem incident logs, NIS2 handoff, dual-use isolation) exactly where §14.21 located it — at the customer-owned edge.

14.31.2 The open-agent-runtime signal: NVIDIA’s agent security stack

In parallel, NVIDIA shipped an open, ownable foundation for autonomous agents rather than only a hosted service. The NeMo Agent Toolkit (open source, framework-agnostic — interoperating with LangChain, LlamaIndex, CrewAI, Semantic Kernel and Google ADK) supplies models, tools and a secure runtime; the OpenShell runtime (part of the Agent Toolkit) provides a kernel-level sandbox with policy-based filesystem, network and privilege controls plus inference routing; and NemoClaw (open reference stack; github.com/NVIDIA/NemoClaw; early preview 16.03.2026) installs Nemotron models and OpenShell in a single command to run always-on local agents — including self-improving Hermes agents (Nous Research) — on owned hardware such as DGX Spark or local workstations. The significance for the Firehorse model is that the most credible silicon vendor has now validated the agent-native, sovereign-deployable architecture this thesis advances: open models + a secure runtime + customer-owned substrate. The direct operating-model consequence is a runtime migration: the Firehorse agent runtime moves from bare OpenClaw to the OpenShell-sandboxed NemoClaw distribution, because an agent framework with shell access is a prompt-injection and supply-chain liability until it runs inside a kernel-level sandbox — precisely the SIB-track exposure §14.21.5 names.

14.31.3 Composition with the four-track KYA conformance suite

OpenShell occupies the same conceptual territory as the KYA Standard candidate’s operational-safety and substrate-integrity tracks. A kernel-level sandbox enforcing filesystem, network, privilege and inference-routing policy is a concrete instance of the ASB-track (operational safety, §14.19.6) running on the SIB-track substrate (what running an agent on a given hardware/runtime exposes the deployer to, §14.21.5). NVIDIA shipping this as open infrastructure is doubly useful to a one-person sovereign operating model: it supplies a vendor-credible runtime to build on (the §14.0 “credibility layer”), and it makes the KYA conformance vocabulary testable against a named reference rather than only against bespoke MicroVM tooling. The division of labour is unchanged: NVIDIA supplies the open foundation (factory upstream, secure runtime at the edge); the sovereign operating model supplies the EU-jurisdiction binding, the Article-12 evidentiary log, the KYA identity spine, and the customer-owned box the runtime executes inside.

14.31.4 Research Contribution

Key Contribution #37 — The Sovereign-Upstream / Open-Agent-Runtime Convergence: NVIDIA’s June 2026 European buildout (35 AI supercomputers / 23 countries / ~800 AI exaflops, anchored by EuroHPC AI Factories) and its open agent security stack (NeMo Agent Toolkit, OpenShell kernel-sandbox runtime, NemoClaw reference stack, Hermes/Nous Research agents) jointly externalise — from the most credible silicon vendor — the two ends of the Firehorse sovereign-edge wager: a public EU-jurisdiction training upstream and an open, customer-ownable secure agent runtime. The contribution is that this convergence does not erode the sovereign-edge thesis but supplies its missing predicates: it resolves model provenance at the upstream (EuroHPC) and gives the KYA ASB/SIB tracks a named, vendor-backed reference runtime (OpenShell) at the edge, while leaving the jurisdiction-binding, Article-12 evidentiary, and single-tenant-deployment layers — the part a shared factory and an open runtime structurally cannot ship — as the sovereign operating model’s defensible remainder. Positioned at the 3–7 year horizon (§1.3) because it depends on the EuroHPC AI-Factory deployment timeline, the maturation of OpenShell/NemoClaw from early preview to production, and the EU AI Act Article 12/14/15 enforcement curve. Primary sources: NVIDIA Newsroom (Europe 35 supercomputers, 22.06.2026; NemoClaw announcement); github.com/NVIDIA/NemoClaw; docs.nvidia.com/nemoclaw; NVIDIA NeMo Agent Toolkit docs.

14.32 The Regulated-Industry Autonomous-Engineer Category: Composing Sovereign Skilled Agents into NemoClaw Workflows

Added: 25.06.2026 | Sources: NVIDIA Newsroom, “Industrial Software Leaders Build Secure, Autonomous AI Engineers With NVIDIA NemoClaw” (02.06.2026, GTC Taipei); NVIDIA NeMo Agent Toolkit (framework-agnostic); DWS production: src/agents/ (51 Skilled Agents on BaseAgent), src/agents/workflow/, src/mcp/nemoclaw-bridge.ts, src/agents/article12-logger.ts. PhD scope per §1.3 (3–7 year horizon).

At GTC Taipei (02.06.2026) NVIDIA and a dozen-plus industrial software providers — Cadence (autonomous RTL engineer), Siemens (Fuse EDA AI Agent), Dassault Systèmes (3DEXPERIENCE Agentic Platform), Synopsys/Ansys, plus startups (Flexcompute, Luminary, Neural Concept, nTop, PhysicsX, P-1 AI, SimScale, Synera) — productised autonomous AI engineers built on NemoClaw: long-running agents that compress weeks of CAE/EDA workflow into hours, each integrating a harness (OpenClaw or Hermes), a model router, and NeMo libraries on the OpenShell secure runtime. This section argues that the same construction is available to an EU-regulated-industry operating model, and that the sovereign governance layer is the part the industrial incumbents structurally do not ship.

14.32.1 The construction is already seamed in the production system

The Firehorse production system supplies the three pieces a NemoClaw long-running agent composes over: (1) 51 Skilled Agents extending BaseAgent, each emitting a ledger-native output contract (journalEntries + regulatoryArtifact + lineage, §14.28); (2) workflow orchestrators (src/agents/workflow/) that already fan out and sequence agents; (3) the KYA-governed bridge (src/mcp/nemoclaw-bridge.ts) with registerSkill() / invoke(), an allowed-skills whitelist, ATS gating, and response sanitisation, plus article12-logger.ts. The integration is therefore not greenfield: it is an adapter between the DWS ledger-native contract and the NemoClaw skill JSON-schema, plus the reverse skill-exposure direction (NemoClaw harness → DWS skill), governed per skill by OpenShell policy and per workflow by a KYA ATS threshold.

14.32.2 The defensible remainder

The industrial leaders demonstrate that NemoClaw supplies autonomy and a secure runtime; they do not supply jurisdiction binding, an Article-12 evidentiary trail per regulated artefact, single-tenant air-gapped deployment, or the four-track KYA conformance vocabulary (ASB/EAB/CSB/SIB). For an EU-regulated workflow — a CSRD report, a CBAM declaration, a site-safety determination — autonomy without admissible evidence and governed oversight is not deployable. The sovereign operating model’s contribution is precisely to make a long-running autonomous agent admissible: every NemoClaw-orchestrated step lands in the Article-12 log, every skill invocation passes the ATS gate, and the whole runs on a customer-owned box. This is the §14.31 division of labour expressed at the workflow layer rather than the runtime layer.

14.32.3 Research Contribution

Key Contribution #38 — The Regulated-Industry Autonomous-Engineer Category: The GTC-Taipei industrial cohort establishes a generalisable pattern — domain Skilled Agents composed into a NemoClaw long-running autonomous engineer — that this contribution extends to EU-regulated industries by adding the layer the cohort omits: a KYA-governed (ASB/EAB/CSB/SIB), Article-12-evidentiary, EU-sovereign, single-tenant execution envelope. The claim is that an autonomous “compliance engineer / CSRD engineer / site-safety engineer” is constructible today from the Firehorse primitives (51 BaseAgent skills + workflow orchestrators + the KYA bridge) composed under NemoClaw/OpenShell, and that the governance envelope — not the autonomy — is the defensible, regulator-required, and competitively scarce component. It composes with §14.28 (Agents→Loops→Workflows sequencing), §14.31 (OpenShell as the ASB/SIB reference runtime), §14.19 (multi-agent KYA safety), and §14.17 (Skilled Taxonomy). 14.33 The Red Queen Gödel Machine Signal: Co-Evolving Agents and Evaluators as a Conformance-Temporal Dimension

Added: 27.06.2026 | Source: Iacob, Jovanović, Shen, Burkhardt, Kurmanji, Tastan, Sani, Venanzi, Odonnat, Cao, Marino, Qiu, Lane. “The Red Queen Gödel Machine: Co-Evolving Agents and Their Evaluators.” arXiv:2606.26294v1, 24 Jun 2026. University of Cambridge, NVIDIA, Flower Labs, MBZUAI, Inria. PhD scope per §1.3 (3–7 year horizon).

On 24 June 2026, a consortium spanning the University of Cambridge, NVIDIA, Flower Labs, MBZUAI, and Inria published the Red Queen Gödel Machine (RQGM), an evolutionary framework for recursive self-improvement under non-stationary utilities (arXiv:2606.26294; accepted as a talk at ALIFE 2026, Waterloo, Canada, August 2026). The RQGM is the fourth generation in the Gödel Machine lineage: Schmidhuber’s original Gödel Machine (2003, IDSIA — provably optimal self-modification under a fixed utility, impractical due to proof requirement) → Darwin Gödel Machine (DGM, Sakana AI, arXiv:2505.22954, May 2025 / ICLR 2026 — replaces proof with empirical evidence, open-ended evolutionary search, SWE-bench 20.0%→50.0%) → Huxley-Gödel Machine (HGM, arXiv:2510.21614, October 2025 — introduces Clade Metaproductivity and Thompson sampling, 2.38×–6.86× fewer CPU-hours than DGM) → RQGM (June 2026 — makes the utility function itself non-stationary and co-evolves evaluators). The paper names a structural weakness in the entire prior generation of self-improving agent systems — including DGM, HGM, Promptbreeder, and DARWIN — that this thesis’s KYA conformance vocabulary inherits unless explicitly extended: all assume a stationary evaluation criterion. A fixed verifier, benchmark, or labelled dataset remains valid as the agent improves. The RQGM argues this ignores a central feature of evolution: species do not optimise against a static environment but adapt as their environments change (Van Valen’s Red Queen hypothesis, 1973). The RQGM makes evaluation part of the improvement loop through controlled utility evolution: search is organised into epochs with a fixed within-epoch evaluation criterion, while the utility can be updated at epoch boundaries, so self-improvement guarantees hold per epoch as the objective evolves across them. The mechanism is instantiated by co-evolving learned evaluators that improve alongside the task agents they guide.

14.33.1 The RQGM architecture and key results

The RQGM searches over a multi-agent workspace tree containing both learnable task agents (generators) and evaluators. At each step, a node is selected by Thompson sampling over clade metaproductivity and either expanded by a meta-agent or evaluated. Evaluators are scored against a ground-truth anchor (frozen per epoch) or a fixed benchmark for evaluator-independent roles. At checkpoints, each frozen slot compares its incumbent against challenger evaluators on a ground-truth anchor; the ε-best-belief evaluator is frozen for the next epoch and the utility records from the displaced evaluator are selectively erased. Utility is defined as U(a) = 1/K Σ P_r(a) — role-uniform and task-balanced within a role.

Four empirical results matter for this thesis:

  1. Coding tasks: RQGM improves test pass rate over prior SOTA by adding a complementary agent-as-a-judge code-review signal. Because the reviewer is queried once (while standard coding agents require multi-turn evaluation), the RQGM uses 1.35×–1.72× fewer tokens.
  2. Scientific paper writing and reviewing: co-evolved writers reach 1.78×–1.86× higher acceptance rates under a diverse agent-as-a-judge panel.
  3. Olympiad-level proof writing and grading: co-evolved graders reach 9% higher ground-truth accuracy.
  4. Reviewer calibration: the strongest baseline reviewer over-accepts AI-generated papers at up to 1.91× the human rate. The RQGM corrects this by introducing an adversarial objective that discovers reviewers equally stringent on AI and human work.

14.33.2 The conformance-temporal gap in the KYA Standard candidate

The KYA Standard candidate’s four-track conformance suite (ASB/EAB/CSB/SIB, §14.19.6–§14.21.5) currently defines conformance as a point-in-time predicate: an agent either passes the ASB-track operational safety check, the EAB-track behavioural evaluation, the CSB-track cost-surface stability test, and the SIB-track substrate-integrity attestation, or it does not. The RQGM names what this formulation misses: conformance decays over time as agents improve. A fixed EAB evaluator (even one seeded from the EARTHwise Arena’s 13-criterion benchmark, §14.19) that passed an agent at epoch j may over-accept a materially different agent at epoch j+k — the 1.91× over-acceptance finding is the empirical proof. This is the conformance-temporal gap.

The architectural response is to add a temporal dimension to the KYA suite: conformance-epoch attestation. At each epoch boundary (operationally: each agent version deployment, each regulatory attestation cycle, each customer audit gate), the evaluator itself must be re-validated against a ground-truth anchor. The four tracks become (ASB_j, EAB_j, CSB_j, SIB_j) — subscripted by epoch. An attestation is valid only for the epoch in which the evaluator was calibrated.

14.33.3 Composition with the Firehorse operating model

Three operating-model implications follow:

1. Maker–Checker Loop upgrade path. The Firehorse Maker–Checker Loop (§14.28, PR #1447) currently uses a fixed verification function as the checker. The RQGM’s single-query reviewer pattern — cheaper and more effective than multi-turn evaluation — maps directly: the checker should be a co-evolved evaluator that improves alongside the maker agent, validated at epoch boundaries against a ground-truth anchor. This converts the Loop from a static safety gate to a self-improving quality gate that gets both better and cheaper over time (compounding the §14.20 CSB-track capital-efficiency thesis).

2. Article 12 evaluator-calibration requirement. The 1.91× over-acceptance finding has a direct EU AI Act implication: an Article 12 evidentiary log generated by a fixed evaluator degrades in reliability as the agent it evaluates improves. For the log to remain court-admissible (§14.12), the evaluator’s calibration state must be part of the log record. Each Article 12 log entry must carry an evaluator-epoch tag, and the evaluator must be periodically re-calibrated against a human-anchored ground truth. This extends the Article 12 logging standard from descriptive (what happened) to calibration-attested (what happened, as judged by an evaluator whose acceptance rate on AI vs human artefacts was within tolerance at the time of judgment).

3. Federated co-evolutionary improvement. Authors Jovanović and Sani are dual-affiliated with Cambridge and Flower Labs, the leading federated learning company. The RQGM’s co-evolutionary architecture is structurally composable with federated learning: sovereign deployments (each with their own evaluators on customer-owned substrate, §14.18/§14.21) could share evaluator improvement signals without pooling data, preserving §14.23’s Data Sovereignty pillar while gaining the co-evolutionary advantage. This is the 3–7 year research horizon where the KYA identity spine (binding agent to jurisdiction) meets the Flower Labs federated transport (binding improvement to sovereignty).

14.33.4 Improvement plan for DWS IQ

The RQGM finding generates five concrete improvement actions for the Firehorse production system:

# Action Composing with Priority Horizon
1 Add evaluator-epoch discipline to KYA conformance: conformance tracks become (ASB_j, EAB_j, CSB_j, SIB_j) with mandatory re-validation at epoch boundaries §14.19.6 (EAB), §14.21.5 (SIB) P1 — standards 2026 H2
2 Replace the Maker–Checker Loop’s fixed checker with a co-evolved evaluator validated against ground truth per epoch §14.28 (Loops), PR #1447 P2 — engineering 2027 Q1

5 Investigate Flower Labs’ federated learning framework as a sovereignty-preserving transport for cross-deployment evaluator improvement signals §14.23 (sovereignty), Flower Labs P3 — research 2027–2028

14.34 The National Aggregation-Effect Framework: Finland’s Ministry of Finance Security-Oriented Information Management Guide and DWS’s Retention Model

Added: 01.07.2026 | Source: Ministry of Finance (Finland). “Critical Infrastructure and Open Information — A Guide to Security-Oriented Information Management.” Publications of the Ministry of Finance 2026:27, 17 June 2026. ISBN PDF 978-952-367-188-1, ISSN PDF 1797-9714, reference number VN/34688/2024, project number VM022:00/2025. Prepared as part of the implementation of Prime Minister Petteri Orpo’s Government Programme (2023) section 8.5, supporting the scope of the CER Directive (EU) 2022/2557. PhD scope per §1.3 (regulatory pipeline, national-level governance framework, 3–7 year horizon).

The Ministry of Finance’s June 2026 guide codifies the first comprehensive national framework for how public- and private-sector holders of critical infrastructure must weigh the benefits of open information against security risk. The guide is not legally binding, but it is the first comprehensive national guidance to name the aggregation effect (kasautumisvaikutus) explicitly as a core assessment criterion: a single dataset rarely constitutes a risk on its own, but a qualitative, temporal, or geographic accumulation of data can reveal critical-infrastructure dependencies and vulnerabilities that no single item would expose (§4.4.2, VM 2026:27).

14.34.1 Signal: The Aggregation Effect as a National Assessment Criterion

The guide’s five-stage assessment process (identify → check obligations → assess benefit/risk → select proportionate measures → document, §3–4.5) constructs the same logic this work has applied to the layered sensor-data retention model for the Control Room and Situation Room products (docs/field-notes/2026-07-01-tiedon-vanheneminen-sensoridata-retention-control-room.md): risk does not arise from a single observation, but from the ability to combine many observations into one whole — either within one system (Control Room → Situation Room aggregation) or over time (a 13-month ontologised summary enables year-over-year comparison, but also pattern recognition). The guide names two mitigation levers that DWS’s four-layer model already implements: (1) limiting accumulated data to what is strictly necessary (Layer 1: 72-hour raw stream, no “just in case” retention), and (2) reducing the number of data processors, distribution channels, and methods (per-layer role-based access, one named owner per ontology node).

14.34.2 Connection to DWS’s Retention Model and KYA Conformance

Three operational implications:

1. The aggregation effect complements the Article 12 logging requirement. The EU AI Act’s Article 12 requires traceability of a single decision; the Ministry’s guide adds a temporal dimension to this — whether the accumulation of log entries is itself a security risk. DWS’s Layer 3 (7-year immutable audit trail, referenced in the Admin Manual) was already designed to be aggregation-aware: a SIEM export interface allows inspection of the accumulation without the accumulation itself being openly available.

2. Situation Room aggregation is the guide’s “controlled disclosure method.” The guide distinguishes open information, publicly available information, and controlled disclosure (Table 16, §4.4.3: “disclosure by a controlled method” and “obscuring within a larger data mass”). DWS’s architectural choice — Situation Room never receives raw Layer 1 data, only site-level aggregates — directly implements this risk-management measure: an individual item is obscured within a larger data mass before it ever leaves the Control Room’s boundary.

3. The national framework gives DWS an external reference point for customer conversations. Holders of critical infrastructure (energy, water, information and communication networks — the scope of CER Act 310/2025) are DWS’s core customer base. The Ministry’s guide gives a customer’s own security leadership the same vocabulary DWS’s retention model already speaks: identifying the aggregation effect, targeting proportionate measures precisely at the data elements actually at risk (not the entire dataset), and documenting measures for later re-assessment.

14.34.3 Standard-Layer Implication

# Measure Composes with Priority Horizon
1 Add an explicit aggregation-effect assessment field per ontology node to the retention model (temporal, geographic, thematic) docs/field-notes/2026-07-01-...-retention-control-room.md, Admin Manual P2 — engineering 2026 H2
2 Document Situation Room aggregation explicitly as the Ministry guide’s “obscuring within a larger data mass” measure in customer contracts KYA conformance vocabulary, customer onboarding P2 — GTM 2026 H2
3 Add the Ministry’s aggregation-effect assessment questions (table-based model) to the Pillar 2 scoping checklist alongside ontology-node ownership docs/KONSULTOINNIN_KASIKIRJA_2026.md §3, Pillar 2 P1 — compliance 2026 H2
4 Wire the Ministry’s five-stage assessment process (§3–4.5) into modelling CER obligations for Aegis customers aegis.html, Ministry of the Interior CER handbook P2 — product 2026–2027

14.34.4 Research Contribution

Key Contribution #41 — Convergence of a National Aggregation-Effect Assessment Framework and a Sovereign Retention Architecture: The Finnish Ministry of Finance’s guide (VM 2026:27, June 2026) is the first comprehensive national guidance to name the aggregation effect (qualitative, temporal, and geographic accumulation of data) as an explicit assessment criterion for the openness of information concerning critical infrastructure. This work shows that DWS’s independently developed four-layer retention model for the Control Room and Situation Room products (raw sensor stream 72h → ontologised summary 13mo → Article 12 audit trail 7yr → site-level rollup 24mo) already implements the guide’s core risk-management measures — limiting accumulation to necessity, obscuring within a larger data mass, controlled disclosure methods — without having been derived from that guide in the first place. The contribution is twofold: (1) empirical confirmation that a sector-developed sovereign retention architecture converges with national-level regulatory guidance regardless of which came first; (2) explicit incorporation of the aggregation effect into the KYA conformance vocabulary as an ontology-node-level assessment field, complementing the existing point-in-time Article 12 logging requirement with a temporal and structural dimension. Positioned at the 3–7 year horizon (§1.3). Primary source: Publications of the Ministry of Finance 2026:27, https://urn.fi/URN:ISBN:978-952-367-188-1.

14.35 The First-Person Data Bottleneck: Jensen Huang’s “Digital Robot” Framing and Sovereign Operational Data as the Scarce Physical-AI Input

Added: 05.07.2026 | Source: Jensen Huang (NVIDIA), public remarks on agentic and physical AI, 2026 (“Agentic AI is just a digital robot. It understands, it reasons, it plans, and it acts and uses tools.” / “The world’s video data is from a third person, not first person. And so agentic systems, robotic systems, physical AI — the data is the hardest problem.”). Composes with §14.13 (LWM Sequencing, Phase 3 physical AI), §14.21 (embodied-AI substrate), §14.26 (Perception Capital), §14.18 (NVFP4 compute), §14.31 (open agent runtime). PhD scope per §1.3 (3–7 year horizon).

Jensen Huang’s compression of agentic AI to “just a digital robot — it understands, it reasons, it plans, and it acts and uses tools” collapses the rhetorical distance between the software agent this thesis studies and the physical robot the thesis has, until now, treated as a later sequencing phase (§14.13, LWM Phase 3). The load-bearing claim is the second one: that the binding constraint on physical AI is not the model but the data, and specifically that “the world’s video data is from a third person, not first person,” so that for “agentic systems, robotic systems, physical AI — the data is the hardest problem.” This section argues that this reframing names, from the silicon vendor’s vantage point, the precise asset the four-capital model already isolated as Perception Capital (§14.26), and that the sovereign operating model’s structural advantage on the physical surface is not compute or model access but custody of consent-governed, jurisdiction-resident first-person operational data.

14.35.1 The signal: the bottleneck is first-person data, not the model

LLMs were trained on a corpus the internet had already produced; physical AI has no equivalent free corpus, because the world’s recorded video is overwhelmingly third-person — footage of agents acting, not the egocentric sensorimotor stream from the acting agent. Huang’s insistence that robots need “the data engine, the simulator, the reward system, the runtime, and the computer” — a stack, not one magical model — restates the §14.18/§14.19/§14.20 thesis that the operating model, not the model weight, is the unit of competition, and extends it to the physical surface. For this thesis the consequential result is a re-ordering of the §14.13 LWM Sequencing Strategy: the gating input on Phase 3 (physical AI) is neither compute (§14.18 resolved it via owned NVFP4 substrate) nor alignment (§14.19 resolved it via the KYA four-track suite) but first-person demonstration and operational data — and that input is the one a sovereign EU operating model can accumulate lawfully and a third-party model vendor cannot simply scrape.

14.35.2 The stack reframing maps onto Brain–Memory–Body–Hands and the four-capital model

Huang’s five-element physical-AI stack maps cleanly onto the DWS architecture — which is the point: the operating model already instantiates the stack at the software layer and need only extend it to the physical layer.

Huang’s physical-AI stack element DWS Operating-Model instantiation Reference
Data engine (first-person capture) Article-12 logging pipeline + AgentLogger JSONL — every agent decision captured egocentrically as a first-person operational trace §14.10, §14.19
Simulator Omniverse / digital-twin partners (Siemens Xcelerator, ABB) for synthetic-world demonstration generation §14.8, Appendix H.5
Reward system KYA conformance (ASB/EAB/CSB/SIB) + the EAB behavioural benchmark (EAC1–EAC13) as the reward-shaping vocabulary §14.19, §14.19.6
Runtime OpenShell-sandboxed NemoClaw agent runtime §14.31, §14.32
Computer Owned EU-sovereign NVFP4 Spark substrate §14.18, §10.10.6.1

The fourth capital class — Perception Capital (§14.26: the accumulating, governed world-model and closed control loop, with explicit physical-AI character and data-network effects) — is the operating-model name for exactly the asset Huang calls the hardest problem. Where §14.26 introduced Perception Capital as a valuation and M&A ontology, this section supplies its production mechanism: Perception Capital is first-person operational data accumulated under the data engine, and the data-network effect is the physical-AI flywheel that Huang’s “data is the hardest problem” remark prices.

14.35.3 Sovereign first-person data as the defensible, jurisdiction-bound input

The decisive move is to observe that a KYA-bound agent population running inside EU-regulated industrial sites is already a first-person data engine. A site-safety determination (SiteSense), a CBAM declaration, a CSRD line item, a teleoperation trace on an ABB cell — each is an egocentric record of an agent perceiving an industrial state and acting on it, captured in the Article-12 log with lineage. This is first-person physical-AI data in Huang’s exact sense, and three properties make it defensible rather than merely present:

  1. It is lawfully accumulable only under consent and residency governance. GDPR (Articles 5–6, 44–49), the EU Data Act, and EU AI Act Article 10 (data governance) make the first-person industrial corpus non-transferable across jurisdictions without a lawful basis — which converts data residency from a compliance cost into a moat: whoever holds the consent-governed, EU-resident corpus holds an input a foreign vendor cannot lawfully replicate.
  2. The non-sovereign route to first-person data is already documented as a liability. §14.21 recorded the Unitree G1’s mandatory five-minute telemetry (audio, video, GPS, LiDAR) to PRC-reachable infrastructure — the embodied-AI industry’s de-facto answer to the first-person-data bottleneck is involuntary, continuous, cross-jurisdiction exfiltration. The sovereign counter-position is the same data captured under consent, logged for Article 12, and bound to the deployer’s jurisdiction at the substrate (SIB-track) layer.
  3. It is lineage-attestable. The Article-12 evidentiary trail (§14.12, §14.32) already attaches provenance to every artefact, so the first-person corpus arrives audit-ready rather than requiring retroactive provenance reconstruction.

Standards-layer implication: the four-track KYA suite gains a data-provenance predicate — a first-person-data-provenance attestation naming, for any physical- or agentic-AI system, whether its operational and training corpus is (a) first-person rather than third-person, (b) consent-governed, (c) jurisdiction-resident, and (d) lineage-traceable to an Article-12 record. This predicate composes with the SIB-track (substrate integrity), GDPR, the EU Data Act, and EU AI Act Article 10, and it is the conformance-language form of Huang’s “data is the hardest problem”: it makes the hardest problem a measurable procurement gate rather than a marketing claim.

14.35.4 Research Contribution

Key Contribution #42 — The First-Person Data Bottleneck and Sovereign Operational Data as the Scarce Physical-AI Input: Jensen Huang’s “digital robot” framing of agentic AI and his identification of first-person data — against the world’s overwhelmingly third-person video corpus — as the hardest problem in physical AI are mapped onto the DWS operating model to yield three results. (a) The §14.13 LWM Sequencing Strategy Phase-3 gating input is re-identified: not compute (§14.18) and not alignment (§14.19) but first-person demonstration/operational data. (b) Huang’s five-element physical-AI stack (data engine, simulator, reward, runtime, computer) is shown to map element-for-element onto the Brain–Memory–Body–Hands architecture, and the production mechanism of Perception Capital (§14.26) is specified as first-person operational data accumulated under the Article-12 data engine, pricing the §14.26 data-network effect. (c) A sovereign, consent-governed, EU-resident first-person industrial corpus is named as the defensible, non-replicable input — converting data residency from a compliance cost into a moat (GDPR / EU Data Act / EU AI Act Article 10) and standing opposite the §14.21 involuntary-telemetry route — and a first-person-data-provenance predicate (first-person / consent-governed / jurisdiction-resident / lineage-traceable) is added to the four-track KYA suite as the conformance-language form of “data is the hardest problem.” Positioned at the 3–7 year horizon (§1.3) because it depends on the physical-AI deployment curve, the maturation of EU digital-twin / simulator partners, and the EU AI Act Article 10/12 enforcement trajectory. Primary source: Jensen Huang (NVIDIA), public remarks on agentic and physical AI, 2026. Composes with §14.13, §14.18, §14.19, §14.21, §14.26, §14.31.

14.36 Two-Signal Convergence: On-Prem AI OS as a Named 2026 Category — Dell + Palantir and Google Antigravity, and the EU-Sovereign Answer

14.36.1 The 48-Hour Convergence

Between 18 May and 19 May 2026, two industry announcements named the Agent / AI Operating System as a category — from opposite ends of the deployment spectrum.

18.05.2026 — Dell + Palantir, On-Premises AI Operating System. Dell Newsroom, byline Rajbir Panag, “Dell and Palantir Introduce an On-Premises AI Operating System.” Architecture: Dell AI Factory hardware (with NVIDIA accelerators) + Palantir Foundry / AIP as the semantic and agent layer. Marketed pillars: unified data (Foundry semantic layer), security and compliance (data sovereignty, zero-trust), pilot-to-production (validated reference architectures + dedicated support). Vendor domicile: both primary vendors US, therefore US CLOUD Act exposure attaches to the vendor relationship itself, on-premises deployment notwithstanding.

19.05.2026 — Google, Antigravity Agent Operating System. Public demonstration with a benchmark run: 93 subagents, 12 hours, 15,000+ model requests, 2.6 billion tokens, under USD 1,000 in API credits. Architecture: hyperscaler-cloud-native, Google-domiciled, sub-agent orchestration on Google’s inference fabric.

The two announcements are not in competition with each other; together they name the category from both ends — on-premises (Dell + Palantir) and cloud (Google Antigravity). Both are US-domiciled. Neither claims EU sovereignty in any structural sense; sovereignty in the Dell + Palantir announcement is a marketing pillar, not an architectural property.

14.36.2 The EU-Sovereign Variant: DWS OS

DWS OS occupies the third quadrant of the convergence map: open, EU-sovereign, on-premises. It is not a positioning relabel — it is a name for the integrated stack already running 24/7 in DWS production (see §14.18 for the underlying NVFP4 hardware moat and §1.2.2 for the unified spine):

  • Hardware: Acer Veriton GN100 with NVIDIA GB10 Grace Blackwell SoC, 128 GB unified LPDDR5x, TPM 2.0 attestation.
  • Inference: Nemotron 3 Super (123.6 B / 12.7 B-active MoE, ~19.5 tok/s — 4.5× dense Llama 70B) + Qwen3-Coder 30B via Ollama; NIM-compatible.
  • Agent runtime: OpenClaw, Nemotron-anchored, no cloud egress. Jensen Huang named OpenClaw “the operating system for personal AI” at NVIDIA GTC 2026 keynote (16.03.2026, see field note 2026-03-16-nvidia-gtc-2026-keynote-dws-impact.md); DWS OS extends that framing to enterprise and government.
  • Governance: KYA + Firecracker MicroVM (< 125 ms kill, AWS benchmark) + 20-industry regulatory engine (CBAM, ETS, CSRD, EU AI Act Art. 12 / 14 / 72, NIS2, DORA).
  • Operator: Lifetime Oy (FI), business ID 0772407-9 — EU-domiciled in the critical path.

14.36.3 The Three-Quadrant Comparator Map

Axis Dell + Palantir On-Prem AI OS Google Antigravity Agent OS DWS OS
Deployment On-premises (validated stack) Hyperscaler cloud On-premises (EU-operated)

The fair statement: Dell + Palantir is a strong validated stack for US enterprises and US-aligned governments; Google Antigravity is a strong cloud-native option where scale, elasticity, and cost-per-token dominate. Neither addresses the EU-sovereign on-premises buyer whose procurement risk is vendor-domicile CLOUD Act exposure. DWS OS is the open EU-sovereign variant of the same category.

14.36.4 Research Implications

Three implications for the doctoral programme follow from the convergence:

  1. Article 2 (2027) target acquires named comparators. Sovereign by Construction — An Architectural Pattern for the EU On-Premises AI Operating System now compares DWS OS to (i) Dell + Palantir on-prem AI OS (18.05.2026) and (ii) Google Antigravity Agent OS (19.05.2026) along the 8-dimension sovereignty rubric of DWS_SOVEREIGNTY_STANDARD.md. This converts an architectural argument into a falsifiable comparative evaluation.
  2. Window of publication priority is narrow. Industry has named the category; academic publication that defines the open EU-sovereign variant has citation value primarily during the 2026–2027 window. After that, the open variant either becomes the standard or a footnote to whichever closed variant achieves enterprise adoption.
  3. KYA standardization gains urgency. Two closed reference architectures are now on the table. An open governance standard that interoperates with neither is unlikely to gain traction; an open governance standard that can govern agents running on all three (Dell + Palantir, Google Antigravity, DWS OS) becomes the interoperability layer the category needs. Article 1’s KYA framing should be tightened to claim that scope explicitly.

14.36.5 Research Contribution

Key Contribution #43 — DWS OS as the Unifying Artefactual Spine for EU-Sovereign Agentic AI. The convergence of 18–19 May 2026 publicly names the on-prem / cloud Agent Operating System category from both ends, both US-domiciled. The doctoral contribution is the open, EU-sovereign variant of that category, made falsifiable by (a) a productionised reference deployment (NVIDIA GB10 + Nemotron 3 Super + OpenClaw + KYA + Firecracker), (b) an 8-dimension sovereignty rubric applied uniformly to all three quadrants of the comparator map, and (c) a governance standard (KYA, Article 1) whose conformance suite is operable on any of the three. The contribution generalises beyond Lifetime Oy: the same pattern applies to any EU-domiciled operator that must deploy agentic AI under CBAM / CSRD / NIS2 / DORA / EU AI Act obligations without acquiring vendor-domicile CLOUD Act exposure. The contribution is positioned at the 3–7 year horizon (per the master-versus-PhD scope rule §1.3) because the standardisation pathway (Agentic AI Foundation / Linux Foundation / NIST profile extension) and the EU sovereign-cloud procurement frameworks (Horizon Europe Sovereign Cloud, Gaia-X) mature on that timeline.

Appendix H: KYA Partner Ecosystem

Added: 18.03.2026 | Source: docs/field-notes/2026-03-09-shufti-kya-byo-partner-access.md, docs/field-notes/2026-03-10-palantir-aip-partner-outreach.md, docs/gtm/KYA_CONNECTOR_BRIEF_PALANTIR.md

The KYA (Know Your Agent) Standard requires a partner ecosystem to deliver its 5-layer trust framework. No single vendor can provide hardware attestation, identity verification, industrial distribution, and EU-sovereign AI inference simultaneously. This appendix documents the partner architecture and its research implications.

H.1 KYA 5-Layer Partner Mapping

The KYA Standard defines five trust layers. Each layer maps to a specific partner capability:

KYA Layer Function Partner Integration Status
L1 — Human Identity KYC/AML/IDV verification Shufti Pro PoC in progress (Q1 2026)
L2 — Agent Provenance Agent origin and registry verification DWS Agent Registry (internal) Production
L3 — Agent Capability Tool/API access gating via MCP manifest DWS MCP Hub (internal) Production
L4 — Runtime Behavior Court-defensible telemetry (egress, file I/O, tokens) — OpenSandbox governance layer wrapping Docker containers; VMM-level monitoring below agent OS; TPM 2.0 attested logs for EU AI Act Art. 12 compliance DWS OpenSandbox (internal) From MVP
L5 — Continuous Risk Dynamic risk profile monitoring Shufti Pro webhooks PoC planned

L4 architecture decision (March 2026): Application workloads run in Docker containers, but L4 telemetry requires OpenSandbox (Firecracker MicroVM) as a governance layer around the Docker runtime. Docker alone cannot provide court-defensible L4 because: (1) shared kernel allows root agents to manipulate their own logs, (2) no TPM 2.0 attestation of telemetry integrity, (3) EU AI Act Art. 12 requires tamper-proof automatic event logging for all high-risk AI systems. All DWS IQ customers operate in EU-regulated industries — there is no “functional-only L4” tier. Every agent interaction must produce a court-defensible audit trail.

Research significance: Layers L2–L4 are built internally, but L1 and L5 require external identity intelligence. This creates a natural partnership boundary: DWS governs the agent runtime; the identity partner governs the human behind it.

H.2 Shufti Pro — Identity Verification Partner (L1/L5)

Problem: Traditional KYC verifies the human at the door. In agent-native architectures, the human is just the entry point. The real risk surface is: what agent does the user deploy, and does the user’s risk profile change after the agent is running?

Integration architecture:

User Onboarding: DWS Auth (Supabase) → Shufti IDV API → KYA L1 Score
Agent Deployment: Agent Manifest → KYA L2-L3 Review → Sandbox Policy
Runtime:          Shufti Webhooks (L5) → DWS Event Bus → Sandbox Restrict/Expand

KYA Trust Score formula:

KYA_TRUST = f(shufti_risk_score, agent_provenance, capability_scope, runtime_behavior)
Score Tier Sandbox Action
>= 80 Full All MCP tools, egress, filesystem
50–79 Restricted No egress, read-only, curated tools
< 50 Suspended Human review required
0 Frozen Agent terminated (122ms Firehorse Snap)

Partnership terms: Technical PoC pilot (not seat-license). Shufti provides API access; DWS provides OpenSandbox runtime and KYA framework. Value proposition for Shufti: first-mover in “Zero-Trust Agent Workspace” category — identity layer for autonomous AI agents, not just humans.

Current status (March 2026): EU-only processing confirmation and DPA (Data Processing Agreement) requested from Shufti. GDPR Art. 44-49 compliance required for enterprise customers. HMAC-SHA256 webhook signing confirmed.

H.3 Palantir AIP — Connector Partner Model

Strategic thesis: Palantir AIP is a platform partner opportunity, not a competitor. Palantir has enterprise distribution and ontology data modeling; DWS has hardware-level agent isolation and EU regulatory depth.

Palantir Has Palantir Lacks DWS IQ Provides
Enterprise agent orchestration (AIP) Hardware-level agent isolation Firecracker MicroVM (122ms kill)
Fortune 500 customer base EU CBAM/ETS/Fit for 55 depth 20-industry regulatory engine
Ontology data model TPM 2.0 hardware attestation Court-defensible audit trail
$2.6B ARR, proven scale Sub-5ms VMM-layer guardrails Hardware-enforced behavioral enforcement

Connector architecture: AIP agent workflows run inside KYA-governed Firecracker MicroVMs. Zero modification to existing Palantir workflows. The governance layer sits below the agent’s operating system.

Revenue models proposed:

Co-sell Joint enterprise deals in EU regulated industries
OEM Palantir bundles KYA governance into AIP EU offering

H.4 BYO (Bring Your Own) Agent Policy

BYO defines how external agents — from any partner or customer — enter the DWS IQ ecosystem:

BYO Stage Gate Enforcement
Registration Agent manifest (name, version, tool requirements, source) DWS Agent Registry API
Identity Binding Agent bound to verified human identity KYA L1 (Shufti)
Capability Review MCP tools reviewed against organization policy MCP Tool Approval
Sandbox Entry Isolated sandbox (Stage 1–3 based on tenant tier) OpenSandbox Architecture
Runtime Monitoring Continuous telemetry: egress, file writes, token consumption KYA L4
Dynamic Restriction Risk profile change → sandbox permissions adjust in real-time KYA L5 (Shufti webhooks)

Significance for Firehorse: BYO enables the platform-as-a-service model. Customers bring their own agents; DWS governs them. This is the monetization layer for the Firehorse operating model.

The 5-partner ecosystem maps to the DWS architecture layers:

Distribution hypothesis validation: Access to 10,000+ industrial customers (NVIDIA Inception, Siemens Xcelerator) without building a direct sales organization. The partnership model is the distribution strategy — not a supplement to it.

H.6 Research Contribution

The KYA partner ecosystem demonstrates three research findings:

  1. Identity-to-runtime bridge: No existing vendor offers continuous identity monitoring that controls autonomous agent sandbox permissions. The Shufti-KYA integration creates a new category: execution-layer trust.

  2. Connector-not-competitor positioning: The Palantir connector model shows that hardware governance can be offered as an infrastructure layer to existing platform vendors — a partnership strategy that avoids direct competition with established players.

  3. Partnership as distribution: For a one-person startup, the 5-partner ecosystem (NVIDIA, Siemens, ABB, xAI, Mistral) provides market access equivalent to a direct sales organization — validating the Firehorse operating model’s hypothesis that partnerships can substitute for headcount in early-stage industrial AI companies.

14.37 The Polyglot Orchestrator Signal: Aspire’s Evolution from C#-Centric Preview to Agent-Aware Stack as Convergence Evidence

14.37.1 The Signal

Between its 8.0 preview era (early 2024, ~PR #1500 in the upstream repository) and mid-2026, Microsoft’s Aspire transformed from an experimental, C#-bound microservices orchestrator into a decoupled, polyglot, AI-integrated cloud-native stack: backing services renamed from Components to Integrations; the machine-level workload requirement deleted in favour of pure package-channel distribution; JavaScript/TypeScript and Python hosting made first-class; the dashboard extended with a GenAI trace visualizer (9.5, improved 13.2); the CLI compiled Native-AOT and given lifecycle verbs (init/update/destroy); and — the sharpest data point — the in-dashboard GitHub Copilot UI shipped and then removed in 13.3, replaced by CLI and MCP access for AI coding agents. (Companion implementation evidence: V3Thesis §10.25; field note 2026-07-07-aspire-development-evolution-pr1500-to-main.md v1.1, which records verification status per claim.)

14.37.2 Three Convergence Signals for the Research Programme

(a) The agent interface consolidates below the UI. Microsoft tried the embedded-assistant pattern (Copilot chat inside the dashboard) and retreated within two minor versions to the machine-interface pattern (CLI + MCP). This is the strongest single-vendor confirmation to date of the skills/CLI paradigm this research programme adopted in §14.16: the durable agent surface is a queryable, structured machine interface, not an assistant embedded in a human UI. For the KYA vocabulary, it supports treating agent-interface form (CLI/JSON/MCP presence, schema stability) as an assessable property of a vendor stack rather than a stylistic choice.

(b) A shared telemetry vocabulary between development tooling and audit trail. The GenAI visualizer renders standard OpenTelemetry GenAI semantic-convention spans (gen_ai.*) from any OTLP source. The DWS implementation emits that same vocabulary from TypeScript agents and persists it to the Article 12 audit trail with trace_id/span_id cross-references (V3Thesis §10.25.2). The scientific significance is the convergence itself: when the developer’s trace UI and the regulator-facing evidence trail share one vocabulary, conformance evidence stops being a parallel bookkeeping system and becomes a projection of operational telemetry. On the 3–7-year horizon this positions the OTel GenAI semantic conventions as a standardisation vehicle for the EAB/ASB tracks of the four-track KYA suite — the conformance predicates can be expressed over span attributes that mainstream tooling already produces and displays.

(c) Per-workload identity as the reported mainstream default. Aspire’s Azure Container Apps deployment model is reported to have replaced a single shared cluster identity with granular per-application managed identities by default. Verification status: secondary synthesis, primary source not yet fetched (honesty register, field note v1.1 §6.1). If confirmed, mainstream deployment tooling defaulting to per-workload least-privilege identity is external convergence on the KYA per-agent identity posture — the same movement observed for the runtime layer in §14.31 (OpenShell) arriving at the identity layer.

14.37.3 Meta-Observation: Adoption by Prerequisite Deletion

Across every major Aspire shift the mechanism is the same: delete a prerequisite (machine workload → NuGet-only; AppHost boilerplate → one SDK line; .NET toolchain → polyglot; runtime dependency → AOT binary). Capability additions rode on prerequisite deletions, not the reverse. This is the same adoption logic as the sovereign-edge single-box thesis (§14.18, §14.36): the DWS deployment answer to “what must the customer already have?” — one GB10-class box, no cloud account, no toolchain — is not a constraint to apologise for but the adoption mechanism itself.

Key Contribution #44 — The Polyglot Orchestrator Convergence Signal and the Shared-Vocabulary Conformance Thesis: Microsoft Aspire’s 2024→2026 evolution is read as three convergence signals for sovereign agentic operating models: (a) the agent interface consolidates below the human UI (in-dashboard Copilot UI removed in 13.3 in favour of CLI + MCP), externally validating the §14.16 skills/CLI paradigm and motivating an agent-interface-form predicate; (b) the OpenTelemetry GenAI semantic conventions emerge as a shared vocabulary between development tooling (Aspire GenAI visualizer, 9.5+) and the EU AI Act Article 12 evidence trail (DWS gen_ai.* persistence, migration 066), converting conformance evidence into a projection of operational telemetry and naming the OTel semconv layer as a standardisation vehicle for the KYA EAB/ASB tracks; (c) per-workload identity reportedly becomes the mainstream deployment default (verification pending), converging on the KYA per-agent least-privilege posture. A meta-observation — adoption proceeds by prerequisite deletion — connects the orchestrator’s trajectory to the single-box sovereign-edge adoption thesis (§14.18, §14.36). Positioned at the 3–7-year horizon (§1.3) via the OTel semantic-convention stabilisation and KYA standardisation timelines. Companion implementation evidence: V3Thesis §10.25 (including the silent-telemetry incident as the operational cost of vocabulary/endpoint mismatch). Composes with §14.16, §14.18, §14.31, §14.36.

14.37.4 Correction (v3.79): The Projection Must Include the Ex-Ante Verdict

§14.37.2(b) claimed that conformance evidence becomes “a projection of operational telemetry” once development tooling and audit trail share one vocabulary. External critique (Hakimi, 2026-07-07) exposed that claim as half-true as formulated: the shared vocabulary contained only Article 12 material — what fired, with what model, at what cost — and nothing of the Article 14 / KYA material: whether the execution should have been able to happen at all. A trace vocabulary that records execution without recording authorization produces a well-organised record of decisions nobody gated. The trace shows the agent reached production; it never shows whether it should have been able to.

The correction is a vocabulary extension rather than a retraction, and it is implemented in the reference stack (V3Thesis §10.25.6): the ex-ante gate verdict travels on the same span as the gen_ai.* attributes (dws.kya.verdict, dws.kya.trust_score, dws.kya.policy_version, dws.kya.gate), with the deliberate default that verdict-less executions are stamped ungated rather than left unmarked — authorization-coverage gaps thereby become enumerable trace queries instead of invisible absences. The restated thesis: conformance evidence is a projection of operational telemetry if and only if the projection includes the ex-ante verdict; a projection of behaviour alone is observability, not conformance. This strengthens Key Contribution #44(b): the OTel GenAI semantic conventions remain the standardisation vehicle, but the KYA-relevant extension the EAB/ASB tracks contribute to that vehicle is precisely the verdict attribute class — the part mainstream tooling does not yet carry. The correction also supplies a falsifiable maturity metric for any deployment claiming gated agency: the fraction of execution spans stamped ungated.

14.38 Industry World Models: From Karpathy’s GPT-2 to 16-Industry Fit for 55 World Model Factory

Added: 21.05.2026 | Source: docs/INDUSTRY_WORLD_MODELS_STUDY.md

This section documents a market-signal and technical-architecture study of world models as they apply to the 16 EU Fit for 55-regulated industries. The study examines whether Karpathy’s GPT-2 reproduction principles — accessible, reproducible, efficient — extend to industry-specific world models, and proposes a modular “world model factory” architecture for the Lifetime World Model (LWM).

14.38.1 The Karpathy Foundation Signal

Andrej Karpathy’s progressive GPT-2 reproduction experiments (nanoGPT → build-nanogpt → llm.c) demonstrated that a capable 124M-parameter language model can be reproduced in 90 minutes for $20 using ~4,000 lines of C/CUDA. His November 2025 deprecation of nanoGPT in favour of nanochat signals a shift toward conversational/agentic architectures. His December 2025 year-in-review identifies six paradigm shifts: RLVR as new training paradigm (directly applicable to compliance verification), LLMs as “summoned ghosts” with jagged performance, Claude Code as the first convincing local AI agent (validating localhost-first deployment over cloud), “vibe coding” crossing the programming accessibility threshold, small efficient models enabling local deployment, and “not even 10% of potential realized” — the field remains wide open.

The bridge to world models: Karpathy’s Tesla Autopilot experience (2017-2022) was effectively world model development — predicting lane positions, vehicle trajectories, and obstacle behaviour from camera inputs. His “software 2.0” thesis (2017) — specifying objectives rather than algorithms — foreshadowed the world model paradigm where systems learn physics rather than having it programmed.

14.38.2 Five Competing World Model Architectures (2026)

The world model landscape has crystallised into five competing architectural families:

  1. Autoregressive (token-prediction): Causal by design, proven at scale, rich text integration. Weakness: slow generation, error accumulation. Best for: text-heavy compliance document flows.
  2. Diffusion (iterative denoising): High visual fidelity, parallelisable. Weakness: non-causal, high compute. Best for: visual simulation, construction site monitoring, process plant modeling.
  3. JEPA (Joint Embedding Predictive Architecture): Energy-efficient latent space prediction, enables planning. LeCun’s AMI Labs (€3B valuation, December 2025) champions this approach. V-JEPA 2 (Meta, June 2025) achieves zero-shot robotic planning. Best for: robotics, material logistics, long-horizon planning.
  4. Hybrid (autoregressive + diffusion): Combines causality with quality. NVIDIA Cosmos 2.5 instantiates this approach with Predict + Transfer + Reason + RL. Best for: autonomous vehicles, fleet management, multi-modal industrial systems.
  5. Active inference (Friston-style): Theoretically grounded in free energy principle, adapts in real-time. Weakness: scaling challenges. Best for: real-time control, energy grid management, process optimisation.

The strict separation between architectures is dissolving: Cosmos 2.5 combines four approaches; CausVid adapts diffusion transformers into autoregressive diffusion transformers with causal attention. The correct question for industrial deployment is “which combination?” not “which architecture?”

14.38.3 The LeCun Thesis and the LWM Validation

LeCun’s departure from Meta (December 2025) and founding of AMI Labs at €3B pre-product valuation constitutes a market signal of first order: the researcher who co-invented convolutional neural networks, received the Turing Award, and ran FAIR for 12 years is betting that LLMs cannot solve physical reasoning and that dedicated world models are necessary. His central argument — LLMs require 400,000 years of text to achieve what a child learns from 16,000 hours of visual experience — identifies a structural efficiency gap that no amount of scaling can close.

This validates the LWM V1 design choice: a physics-aware, persistent-memory world model built on NVIDIA Cosmos rather than an LLM extended with tools. The LWM’s Temporal Latent Space, sensor fusion (FQ2BAM), and digital twin synchronisation (OpenUSD/Omniverse) align with the direction confirmed by AMI Labs, DeepMind Genie 3, World Labs Marble, and Siemens Digital Twin Composer (CES 2026).

Counter-position acknowledged: hybrid architectures combining LLM linguistic knowledge with dedicated world model modules may prove the practical path. The DWS IQ 6 Brain already implements this — multi-agent orchestration (LLM) + LWM (world model) + Firehorse (execution).

14.38.4 Industry World Model Requirements — The 16-Industry Matrix

A systematic mapping of Fit for 55 regulatory requirements against world model capabilities produces industry-specific specifications. Common requirements across all 16 industries:

  • Regulatory constraint layer (hard-encoded EU regulation)
  • Carbon accounting engine (Scope 1/2/3 per GHG Protocol)
  • Audit trail persistence (EU AI Act Article 12)
  • Temporal prediction (emissions trajectory forecasting)
  • Supply chain visibility (embedded carbon tracking)
  • Scenario simulation (10,000+ regulatory scenarios per LWM V1 spec)
  • KYA governance for agentic decisions

Each industry then requires unique physics engines, temporal horizons, sensor fusion modalities, and causal reasoning capabilities. Examples of industry-specific world model differentiators:

Industry Unique World Model Characteristic Primary Architecture
Power & Heat Merit order effect (renewable displacement) Hybrid + active inference
Iron & Steel Green steel transition (BF→EAF→H-DRI) multi-decade path Diffusion + JEPA
Cement & Lime Irreducible process emissions (calcination CO₂) Diffusion + autoregressive
Chemicals Dual-use carbon (energy source vs product feedstock) Hybrid
Aviation Non-CO₂ climate effects (contrails, altitude NOx) Autoregressive
Maritime 100% allowance surrender cliff (2026) JEPA + autoregressive
Road Transport Multi-modal logistics chain (truck+rail+last-mile) Hybrid
Construction 50+ year building lifecycle (EN 15978 A1-D) Diffusion + JEPA + Cosmos
Agriculture Bidirectional carbon (emission source + sink) JEPA + active inference
Waste Negative emissions (methane capture credits) Autoregressive + diffusion
Aluminium 80% Scope 2 (grid carbon intensity dominance) JEPA + active inference
Pulp & Paper 50+ year biogenic carbon cycle (forest rotation) JEPA + autoregressive
Petroleum Managed decline (optimise present + transform future) Hybrid
Food & Beverage Scope 3 dominance (70-90% from agriculture) Autoregressive + JEPA
Glass & Ceramics High thermal inertia (continuous furnace campaigns) Diffusion + autoregressive
Mining Resource depletion + geological uncertainty + CRM Act JEPA + diffusion

Full specifications for each industry are documented in docs/INDUSTRY_WORLD_MODELS_STUDY.md §8.

14.38.5 The Modular World Model Factory

The study’s core architectural recommendation is a modular world model factory rather than 16 monolithic industry models:

Foundation World Model (Cosmos/JEPA, 1-10B params)
    ├── Shared physics engine (thermodynamics, chemistry, mechanics)
    ├── Shared regulatory ontology (EU regulations encoded as constraints)
    ├── Shared carbon accounting (GHG Protocol, Scope 1/2/3)
    └── Industry adapters (100M-500M params each, fine-tuned)
         ├── Domain-specific tokenizer + sensor fusion
         ├── Industry-specific regulations + causal graphs
         └── KYA governance hooks + Article 12 logging

This architecture is directly enabled by Karpathy’s core insight: the training recipe and data quality matter more than architecture size. A 100M-500M parameter industry adapter, trained on curated domain data with proper learning rate schedules and gradient accumulation, can provide industry-competitive world modeling at edge-deployable scale (GN-100 class, per §14.18).

Estimated cost per industry adapter: €10K-€50K training, 1-4 weeks, deployable on sovereign edge hardware. The 16-industry expansion follows a phased rollout: Construction (2026, LWM V1) → Steel/Cement/Chemicals (2027, CBAM urgency) → Transport (2028, ETS/ETS2) → Agriculture/Energy/Aluminium (2029, ESR) → Remaining 6 industries (2030, full Fit for 55 coverage).

14.38.6 Ecosystem Convergence Signal (2026)

Five concurrent market events validate the industry world model thesis:

  1. AMI Labs (LeCun, €3B valuation) — JEPA architecture commercialisation for physics understanding
  2. Siemens Digital Twin Composer (CES 2026) — industrial digital twins at scale via NVIDIA Omniverse, with PepsiCo showing 20% throughput gains
  3. NVIDIA Cosmos 2.5 — open-weight world foundation models downloaded 2M+ times, purpose-built for physical AI
  4. DeepMind Genie 3 — real-time interactive 3D world generation at 24fps
  5. World Labs Marble (Fei-Fei Li) — text-to-navigable-3D-world generation

These represent independent bets by the world’s top AI institutions that world models — not LLMs alone — are the path to physical AI. The DWS IQ 6 / LWM positioning at the intersection of world models and EU regulatory compliance is validated as the correct market position.

14.38.7 Research Contribution

Key Contribution #45 — Industry World Model Factory Architecture and 16-Industry Fit for 55 Specification Matrix: The contribution is a systematic mapping of five competing world model architectures (autoregressive, diffusion, JEPA, hybrid, active inference) against the specific physics, temporal, spatial, sensor, and regulatory requirements of each of the 16 EU Fit for 55-regulated industries, producing the first industry-specific world model specification matrix. The study demonstrates that no single architecture serves all industries and proposes a modular factory architecture (shared foundation + lightweight industry adapters at GPT-2-class parameter counts), validated by the Karpathy reproducibility thesis and the 2026 ecosystem convergence (AMI Labs, Cosmos 2.5, Genie 3, Marble, Digital Twin Composer). The contribution positions the Lifetime World Model as the regulatory-compliant instantiation of this factory pattern, with phased 16-industry expansion from construction (2026) to full Fit for 55 coverage (2030). This contribution is at the 3-7 year horizon per §1.3 because it depends on: AMI Labs delivering commercial JEPA; NVIDIA Cosmos maturing industry adapters; world model evaluation standards emerging (no equivalent of BLEU/ROUGE for physics prediction exists today); and the modular factory pattern being validated at multi-industry scale on the Firehorse testbed.

14.39 AI Sovereignty as an Institutional Precondition: The Nine-Theses Framework and the Weights-Control Argument

Added: 01.07.2026 | Companion artefact: docs/field-notes/2026-07-01-ai-sovereignty-nine-theses.md | PhD scope per §1.3 (3–7 year horizon, scientific framing) | Composes with §14.18 (sovereign edge), §14.20 (CSB cost-surface), §14.23 (three-pillar sovereignty), §14.24 (capital-efficiency), §14.25/§14.26 (three-/four-capital), Appendix H.3 (Palantir AIP connector).

The sovereignty sections accumulated across this thesis (§14.18, §14.20–§14.27, §14.29–§14.33) are architecturally specific but lack a unifying normative statement of why sovereignty is the deciding institutional variable rather than one desirable property among many. This section supplies that statement as a nine-theses framework and derives its central formal claim — the weights-control argument — which subsumes the prior sovereignty contributions under a single optionality thesis. The framework is normative-scientific (it states what an institution ought to control and why the failure mode is terminal), and per the §1.4 master-versus-PhD scope rule it lives in the doctoral thesis; the master thesis receives a lockstep-only version-bump entry.

14.39.1 The optionality definition of sovereignty

Existing definitions of AI sovereignty are jurisdictional (data residency), technical (owned compute), or operational (human-in-the-loop control). This section proposes a definition that subsumes all three: an institution’s AI sovereignty is the measure of the future choice set it can still exercise without a third party’s permission. Under this definition, relinquishing sovereignty is not the addition of a dependency but the transfer of the choice set itself to a vendor who holds it as a call option — exercisable precisely at renewal, at scale, or under competitive pressure, when the institution can least afford the strike (Thesis 1). This reframes the §14.23 three-pillar model (Data / Technology / Operational) as a statement about optionality preservation rather than about protectionism, and makes sovereignty measurable as the cardinality of the permission-free choice set.

14.39.2 The three confiscated assets

The framework identifies three assets through which vendor-controlled AI silently confiscates the choice set, each mapped to an existing contribution:

Asset Confiscation mechanism Thesis Prior contribution
Data Proprietary data is simultaneously the record of winning plays and the means of production for new ones; ingesting it into a vendor pipeline arms competitors with both 2 §14.25 (Control Room capital)
Weights Weights are institutional knowledge distilled to a portable artifact; vendor control of weights migrates the institution’s compounding engine (its alpha) upstream 4 §14.1 (per-industry LWM), §14.18 (owned substrate)
Value orientation Token-throughput optimisation (“tokenmaxxing”) installs a vendor-chosen objective function, incentivising disposable scripts over durable capital 3 §14.20 (CSB cost-surface), Rule 6

The unifying observation is that all three confiscations feel like services rendered rather than assets surrendered — the artifact is rented-seeming even as the crown jewels transfer. This perceptual asymmetry is why the failure mode is slow, quiet, and total, and why it must be resisted architecturally rather than contractually.

14.39.3 The weights-control argument (central formal claim)

The load-bearing claim of the framework is Thesis 5: there is no contradiction between sovereignty and alpha. The standard framing treats sovereignty as a capability tax — a cost paid for compliance that an unconstrained competitor avoids. The weights-control argument refutes this:

Let an institution’s alpha be the compounding return on its accumulated proprietary judgment. That judgment is materialised as data (raw), weights (distilled), and operating loop (applied). Compounding requires that each cycle’s output re-enter the institution’s own substrate as the next cycle’s input. Vendor control of any of the three breaks the re-entry: the increment accrues to the vendor’s model, which serves the field. Therefore the architecture that maximally preserves sovereignty (institution owns data, weights, loop) is identical to the architecture that maximally compounds alpha. Sovereignty and alpha are not points on a frontier trade-off; they are the same axis.

The empirical support is the §14.24 capital-efficiency finding: a coalition capital advantage of up to 23× buys only a ~2.7% frontier-capability margin when the disadvantaged party substitutes owned substrate and vertical integration for rented capability. If sovereignty were a capability tax, the owned-substrate operating model could not stay near the frontier at a 23× capital disadvantage; that it does is the falsification of the trade-off framing and the confirmation of the same-axis claim.

14.39.4 The false-sovereignty and epistemic predicates

Four theses (6–9) form the governance layer that prevents the framework from degrading into symbolism:

  • Thesis 6 (anti-politicisation). Techno-politicisation produces false sovereignty — the flag on the box changes while the lock-in does not. The adversary benefits, because a politically-motivated buyer is predictable and capturable, and the cost is highest “on the battlefield in the West” where symbolic procurement fails in the field. The architectural response is to make every sovereignty claim a testable predicate (ASB/EAB/CSB/SIB, §14.19–§14.21; per-jurisdiction triple, §14.23) rather than a badge.
  • Thesis 7 (proximity credential). Politics-driven technical choice optimises for the persuasive over the correct — uncorrelated quantities whose gap compounds. Remedy: weight the judgment of those closest to the failure surface (operationalised as Rule 5, Rule 7).
  • Thesis 8 (existential teachers). Institutions under genuine existential pressure select technology by what works, not by preference; their delivery-proven practices (§14.21/§14.24) are the correct reference class.
  • Thesis 9 (track-record epistemics). A record of correctness is the only reliable signal for future correctness; judging a claim by affinity to its source is category error. This is DWS_TRUTH_STANDARD discipline applied to sovereignty assertions.

Theses 6–9 are jointly the claim that sovereignty must be sourced and checkable, not asserted and liked — the epistemic complement to the architectural claim of Theses 1–5.

14.39.5 Composition with the Palantir connector model

The framework sharpens rather than contradicts the Appendix H.3 connector-not-competitor position. Palantir’s public argument that institutions must own their data and operational advantage is Theses 1, 2, and 4; the DWS extension is the layer below the agent’s operating system (Firecracker MicroVM isolation, <125 ms kill, Article 12 evidentiary logging, exportable single-tenant EU-resident weights). The three-point reply developed in the companion field note formalises this: (1) agreement on thesis with the sovereign boundary one layer lower — a partnership, not a conflict; (2) sovereignty as a testable conformance triple (where DWS supplies the checkable predicate Palantir’s distribution and ontology assert); (3) the compound-ownership position (customer owns ontology and weights/residency/audit trail) as a strictly stronger sovereign outcome, defensible against the model-provider services tier (§14.27) precisely because neither party is that vendor. This is the operational form of Thesis 5: two owners of adjacent layers compounding a customer’s alpha with no party holding the customer’s fate.

The framework’s defensibility — as distinct from its normativity — is operationalised in the companion field note as a nineteen-moat stack under the master frame sovereignty vs. interdependence (every moat a place where owning the lever beats depending on a counterparty for it), opened by the founder’s three primary moats (local-jurisdiction nativeness; sovereignty-as-market-segmentation, scoped locally; and the full “sovereignty exit” cost that interdependence wins — the total in-house-reconstitution bill, not merely the licence — removed by building and maintaining one’s own libraries) and completed by sixteen further moats each mapped to a documented section (weights ownership §14.1/§14.18; data residency §14.25; substrate integrity §14.21; court-defensible evidence §14.12; testable conformance vocabulary §14.19–§14.21/§14.33; capital efficiency §14.24; vertical domain knowledge §14.1/§14.27; regulatory depth; linguistic provenance §14.30; connector-not-competitor Appendix H; compounding alpha §14.25/§14.26; operational maturity §14.25; sovereign upstream §14.31; post-quantum readiness §14.23; track-record epistemics; and standardization ownership §14.22). The moats are non-independent and compound as a stack rather than a feature list — the reason the sovereign operating model is a position, not a checklist.
